# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-11

## Time Series

| **[COMET: Codebook-based Online-adaptive Multi-scale Embedding for Time-series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01635v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time-series anomaly detection aims to identify abnormal temporal patterns in complex real-world systems. However, robust unsupervised time-series anomaly detection remains challenging because anomalies can manifest over different temporal ranges and emerge from interactions among variables, while distribution shifts can cause normal patterns to drift over time. To address these challenges, we propose Codebook-based Online-adaptive Multi-scale Embedding for Time-series anomaly detection (COMET), a unified framework with three tightly coupled components. First, Multi-scale Patch Encoder learns correlation-aware patch embeddings by combining variable-specific temporal dynamics with shared inter-variable structure across multiple patch scales. Second, Vector-Quantized Coreset quantizes these embeddings into representative normal prototypes, which are used to detect anomalies through a dual score combining quantization error and density-aware memory distance. Third, Online Codebook Adaptation leverages the same codebook structure to identify reliable normal samples from training-activated codebook entries, and then updates the prototypes through contrastive learning at inference time. Experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that COMET achieves the best performance on 39 out of 45 evaluation metrics compared to seven baselines, showing robust performance across diverse evaluation protocols.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster). 31 pages, 5 figures, 23 tables. Code: https://github.com/JINU6497/COMET</p></details> |
| **[VETime: Vision Enhanced Zero-Shot Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16681v4)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series anomaly detection (TSAD) requires identifying both immediate Point Anomalies and long-range Context Anomalies. However, existing foundation models face a fundamental trade-off: 1D temporal models provide fine-grained pointwise localization but lack a global contextual perspective, while 2D vision-based models capture global patterns but suffer from information bottlenecks due to a lack of temporal alignment and coarse-grained pointwise detection. To resolve this dilemma, we propose VETime, the first TSAD framework that unifies temporal and visual modalities through fine-grained visual-temporal alignment and dynamic fusion. VETime introduces a Reversible Image Conversion and a Patch-Level Temporal Alignment module to establish a shared visual-temporal timeline, preserving discriminative details while maintaining temporal sensitivity. Furthermore, we design an Anomaly Window Contrastive Learning mechanism and a Task-Adaptive Multi-Modal Fusion to adaptively integrate the complementary perceptual strengths of both modalities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that VETime significantly outperforms state-of-the-art models in zero-shot scenarios, achieving superior localization precision with lower computational overhead than current vision-based approaches. Code available at: https://github.com/yyyangcoder/VETime .</p></details> |  |
| **[ERP Event Logs: A Canonical Form for KPI Time-Series Extraction and Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09903v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise resource planning (ERP) systems record operations as transactions spread across hundreds of normalized relational tables. Extracting an operational key performance indicator (KPI) from this schema requires a separate, bespoke join for almost every metric. We show that an event log, a canonical (case, activity, timestamp) representation derived from the same tables, collapses this heterogeneity into one flat structure, from which KPI families reduce to reusable operations after a one-time mapping. Using standardized event logs built from hundreds of SAP S/4HANA customers under a shared activity ontology, we define three families of process-derived KPIs (volumes, durations, and rates) and extract them uniformly across customer systems for two common end-to-end organizational processes, order-to-cash and procure-to-pay. We normalize rates and screen series for minimum coverage to support comparison across customers of very different sizes. We then characterize heterogeneity across industries and tenants using descriptors derived directly from the canonical form, before analyzing the resulting KPI time series. The same process executes heterogeneously across customers and industries, yet this shared representation enables direct comparison of sales- and procurement-side signals, including lead times and order arrivals. Using this shared representation, we characterize which KPI families are forecastable and find a recurring ordering from volumes through durations to rates across both processes and a highly heterogeneous customer base; a pretrained time-series foundation model (Chronos) is competitive with classical baselines such as ARIMA and ETS on rate and duration series, while classical models retain an edge on volumes.</p></details> | <details><summary>accep...</summary><p>accepted in D2AI at IEEE ICDM 2026. Version 2 corrects a mislabeled rate family in the forecastability figure (the "receipt rate" row is the PO change rate); clarifies the KPI/rate definitions in Sections I, IV and Table I. Results unchanged</p></details> |
| **[Deep learning for structural health monitoring: An application to heritage structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.10351v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Thanks to recent advancements in numerical methods, computer power, and monitoring technology, seismic ambient noise provides precious information about the structural behavior of old buildings. The measurement of the vibrations produced by anthropic and environmental sources and their use for dynamic identification and structural health monitoring of buildings initiated an emerging, cross-disciplinary field engaging seismologists, engineers, mathematicians, and computer scientists. In this work, we employ recent deep learning techniques for time-series forecasting to inspect and detect anomalies in the large dataset recorded during a long-term monitoring campaign conducted on the San Frediano bell tower in Lucca. We frame the problem as an unsupervised anomaly detection task and train a Temporal Fusion Transformer to learn the normal dynamics of the structure. We then detect the anomalies by looking at the differences between the predicted and observed frequencies.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bayesian precision factor models for high-dimensional macroeconomic time series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12368v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Series in a macroeconomic panel are almost all correlated with one another, which makes correlation a poor guide to which pairs are directly linked. For example, Treasury rates at different maturities are all highly correlated, yet a short and a long rate may be related only through the rates at maturities between them; similarly, sectors of the economy such as output, employment, prices, and interest rates may be related directly or only through other series. Both are questions about the inverse covariance matrix. Recovering it from quarterly data is difficult: contemporaneous dependence is entangled with temporal dynamics, the innovations remain densely dependent once those dynamics are removed, and the number of series is comparable to the number of quarters observed. We propose VAPOR (Vector Autoregressive Precision factOR), a Bayesian model that estimates vector autoregressive dynamics jointly with a factor-structured innovation precision matrix, inferring the partial correlations directly from the fitted precision rather than imposing a zero pattern. Applied to 100 quarterly FRED-QD series, VAPOR learns from the data that each Treasury rate is directly related only to the rates at adjacent maturities, places all 26 selected dependencies within a single FRED-QD group, and attains the lowest held-out prediction error among the estimators compared.</p></details> | <details><summary>75 pa...</summary><p>75 pages (35 main text and references, 40 supplementary material), 11 figures</p></details> |
| **[Learning Evidence-Grounded Reasoning for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12285v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-driven time series forecasting has become increasingly promising by leveraging contextual features to generate future values directly or refine forecast priors. However, incorporating contextual features alone does not ensure that models can identify which features matter for forecasting and understand what they suggest about future dynamics. We argue that the fundamental challenge is how to extract predictive evidence from contextual features and when and how to leverage it to guide forecasting decisions. In this paper, we propose CastReasoner, an evidence-grounded reasoning model for context-aware time series forecasting. Specifically, CastReasoner uses a structured chain of thought (CoT) to extract predictive evidence from contextual features, infer the future dynamics suggested by this evidence, and assess these dynamics against the forecast prior to guide adaptive forecasting decisions. To enable this capability, we design a reasoning-centric two-stage post-training framework including supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). First, we synthesize reasoning trajectories through controllable multi-teacher guidance and select high-quality ones using a novel student-aware difficulty metric. Second, we design fine-grained multi-objective rewards to further improve reasoning and forecasting. To address heterogeneous learning needs across domains in both stages, we introduce a convergence-guided data balancing strategy. Through this two-stage training, CastReasoner improves both reasoning quality and predictive accuracy. Extensive experiments across multi-domain forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that CastReasoner achieves superior forecasting performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Foundation Models for Zero-Shot Time Series Anomaly Detection: Leveraging Synthetic Data and Relative Context Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21190v6)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) is a critical task, but developing models that generalize to unseen data in a zero-shot manner remains challenging. Existing foundation models for TSAD often rely on reconstruction-error scoring at inference time, which can miss subtle anomalies that are well reconstructed and can falsely flag complex but normal patterns in unseen domains. We introduce TimeRCD, a foundation model for TSAD built on Relative Context Discrepancy (RCD), a pre-training paradigm that trains the model to detect anomalies by comparing a query pattern with its surrounding context. This relational formulation, implemented with a standard Transformer architecture, enables the model to infer normality from the input context rather than relying on fixed global normal patterns. We further construct a large-scale synthetic corpus with context-dependent anomaly labels to provide supervised pre-training signals for RCD. Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that TimeRCD outperforms existing general-purpose and anomaly-specific foundation models in most zero-shot TSAD settings, while remaining competitive with dataset-specific full-shot baselines. These results provide empirical evidence that RCD is an effective direction for building robust and generalizable TSAD models.</p></details> |  |
| **[ROMAN: A Multiscale Routing Operator for Convolutional Time Series Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02577v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce ROMAN (ROuting Multiscale representAtioN), a deterministic operator for time series that maps temporal scale and coarse temporal position into an explicit channel structure while reducing sequence length. ROMAN builds an anti-aliased multiscale pyramid, extracts fixed-length windows from each scale, and stacks them as pseudochannels. ROMAN provides a training-free mechanism to control the inductive bias of downstream models: it can reduce temporal invariance, make temporal pooling implicitly coarse-position-aware, and expose multiscale interactions through channel mixing, while often improving computational efficiency by shortening the processed time axis. We formally analyze the operator and evaluate it as preprocessing for four convolutional classifiers: MiniRocket, MultiRocket, a standard CNN-based classifier, and a fully convolutional network (FCN) classifier. Synthetic mechanism studies that isolate coarse position, long-range correlation, multiscale interaction, and full positional invariance show that ROMAN behaves as intended, and comparisons with coordinate augmentation and single-scale routing distinguish the benefits of positional routing from those of the multiscale cascade across the evaluated backbones. On long-sequence subsets of the UCR and UEA archives, ROMAN's effect on accuracy varies across datasets while its effect on efficiency is often favorable. At \(S=4\) on UCR, accuracy changes are positively correlated across the pooled backbones, consistent with the proposed role of task structure. We give practical guidance for choosing the operator's two hyperparameters and illustrate its use on very long sequences, where it is both more accurate and several times cheaper, and as a source of representation diversity for ensembles. The ROMAN operator library is available at \href{https://github.com/gon-uri/ROMAN}{github.com/gon-uri/ROMAN</p></details> |  |
| **[Do Time-Series Foundation Models Forecast Index-Futures Returns? A Preregistered Evaluation of Models, Metrics, and Input Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12141v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We ask whether the apparent forecasting skill of frozen time-series foundation models (TSFMs) on intraday index-futures returns is incremental skill. In a pre-registered evaluation on E-mini Nasdaq-100 (NQ) and E-mini S&P 500 (ES) futures, a common panel of five zero-shot models (Chronos-Bolt, Chronos-2, TimesFM 2.5, Moirai 2.0 and TiRex), plus separate experiments with Kronos, is compared with a no-change forecast at 5-120 minutes, using native prices, log returns, volatility-standardised returns and a volume clock as inputs. On a blind set opened once, 0 of 160 model-representation-horizon-market cells beat no-change, and a Hansen SPA test gives p = 1.000 on both markets; a later retrospective hold-out, scored as a descriptive check, gives 0 of 120. Input representations change forecast magnitude but not accuracy, and a transformation-aware ridge regression does as well. Several standard measures nevertheless suggest skill. Price-level correlation exceeds 0.9996, but no-change scores higher. Within-day RankIC across forecast origins rewards a trivial revert-to-mean rule (+0.35 at 30 minutes); we derive the expected within-day covariance under a random walk in closed form, and a sign-randomised null reproduces about 95% of the observed value. One model beats HAR for 5-minute volatility, but not the EWMA its input already contains. On strongly planted synthetic signals the models recover part of the signal at 5 minutes, and recovery deteriorates at 30 minutes. Benchmark-level success of TSFMs should therefore not be assumed to carry over to low-signal domains such as intraday returns.</p></details> |  |
| **[Embedded Decomposed Transformer for explainable multivariate time series forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.12227v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series forecasting is an important problem with diverse real-world applications. This paper introduces the Embedded Decomposed Transformer (EDformer) for multivariate time series forecasting. EDformer decomposes the input signal into seasonal and trend components using two complementary strategies: time-domain Average Pooling (AP) based on moving-average smoothing and Frequency-Aware (FA) decomposition based on low-pass filtering in the Fourier domain. The reconstructed multivariate seasonal component is embedded across inverted dimensions, after which attention captures cross-variate correlations and a feed-forward network learns nonlinear representations for each variable. The trend component is forecast through projection and combined with the seasonal prediction to produce the final forecast. We evaluate both decomposition variants on several real-world datasets, showing that EDformer achieves competitive performance, while their relative effectiveness varies across datasets, experimental settings, and temporal characteristics. We further conduct an ablation study isolating the contribution of decomposition, inverted embedding, and the frequency cutoff ratio; a hyperparameter sensitivity analysis over learning rate and batch size; and a statistical significance analysis using Friedman and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests to substantiate the reported performance differences. Finally, model explainability techniques are explored to improve interpretability and provide insights into the features and time steps influencing the forecasting results.</p></details> |  |
| **[Conformal Prediction for Multi-Step Forecasting: Conditional Coverage and Sharp Score Calibration Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12004v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty quantification in prediction is a central problem in statistics and machine learning. Conformal prediction provides a flexible approach to constructing prediction sets with valid marginal coverage under exchangeability. In this paper, we investigate conformal prediction for both one-step and multi-step forecasting from a single stationary time series, addressing temporal dependence and the additional challenges of joint prediction across multiple horizons. We propose a kernel-based framework, called Time series Residual Analysis for Calibration with Kernels (TRACK), for estimating conditional quantiles of residual scores and constructing prediction sets from rolling-window forecasters. The framework accommodates heterogeneous uncertainty across horizons and accounts for dependence induced by rolling estimation, while allowing predictor memory and forecast horizons to grow with the sample size. Our finite-sample bounds quantify how temporal dependence and overlapping score windows affect calibration and coverage. We further derive bounds for conditional quantile estimation and pointwise conditional coverage discrepancy. The guarantees extend to more general predictors that admit an accurate rolling-window approximation. We also derive matching minimax bounds over classes of known score maps, clarifying how overlapping score windows affect calibration accuracy. Experimental results from simulations and applications to wind energy generation and bike sharing demand show that our methods improve regional conditional coverage while maintaining competitive interval widths.</p></details> | 32 pages |
| **[CHORD: Multi-resolution Compression for Multimodal Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11913v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wearables, mobile devices, and IoT platforms generate multimodal time-series data continuously and at scale, making efficient storage and transfer a primary concern. The same recordings are reused after collection by many downstream models, often at different temporal resolutions, so a compressed representation should be small, useful across resolutions, and produced at a bit rate known in advance. Prior work meets parts of this: symbolic and error-bounded compressors reconstruct accurately but fix resolution at compression time, learned codecs target one signal domain, and discrete tokenizers reconstruct without accounting for rate. We introduce CHORD, a coarse-to-fine compressor for heterogeneous multimodal time series. A residual cascade orders each payload from broad to fine temporal scales and synthesizes corrections through learned shapelet-inspired atoms. A shared pathway supplies a reusable synthesis basis across modalities, while private pathways retain capacity for sensor-specific morphology. Finite scalar quantization fixes the bit width of every modality and scale, so payload size is known before encoding, and scale dropout trains each coarse-to-fine prefix to be a valid reconstruction operating point. Across seven wearable-sensing and forecasting benchmarks, CHORD stays within 0.62 points of uncompressed classification accuracy on average at the 8x target, the smallest gap of any compressed method, with all resolutions decoded from one payload.</p></details> |  |
| **[Quantifying Long-Range Dependence in Object-Valued Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24153v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object-valued time series, including distributions, covariance matrices, networks, and compositions, lack the linear structure required by conventional autocovariance-based definitions of long memory. We develop an intrinsic framework for defining and estimating long memory in metric spaces of negative type. An isometric embedding yields a centered Hilbert-valued process, and the trace of its lag-covariance operator provides a signed, additive measure of temporal dependence. Crucially, this trace equals the difference between the marginal mean pairwise distance and expected lagged distance, so the framework and its estimators use only distances between the original objects. We define the memory parameter through the nonsummable decay of this trace and show that it coincides with the usual parameter in Hilbert-valued settings. Estimation uses Bartlett aggregates of distance-based lag measures. Estimating the common marginal distance from the same dependent sample induces a common-centering bias in these aggregates. We derive its finite-sample form and propose iterated block-difference corrections, log-ratio and multi-bandwidth log-slope estimators, and a localized self-consistency refinement. We establish consistency, document substantial bias reduction in simulations, and find long-memory evidence in foreign-exchange return distributions and U.S. electricity-generation compositions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Optimal Radial Weighting for Robust Mutual Fund Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11762v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Identifying skilled mutual funds requires methods that distinguish positive alphas from sampling variation when many funds are evaluated simultaneously. Heavy-tailed returns and dependence across funds complicate this task. We propose a robust selection procedure based on radially weighted alpha estimation in linear factor pricing models. The method uses both the directions and lengths of standardized residuals, estimates marginal scales from pairwise residual differences, and accounts for the estimation of factor loadings. Within the proposed class, inverse-norm weighting minimizes the asymptotic variance of the alpha estimators and maximizes the local asymptotic power of individual tests at a common significance level. For radial weights fixed conditional on the factor design, we establish asymptotic false discovery rate control under elliptically symmetric errors and show that the proportion of sufficiently strong positive-alpha funds selected tends to one. The theory accommodates comparable cross-sectional and time-series dimensions and nonvanishing alphas. We also develop a smoothed implementation and assess its finite-sample properties. Simulations show higher true discovery proportions than least-squares and spatial-sign methods under heavy-tailed distributions and improved numerical stability from smoothing. An application to U.S. equity mutual funds examines the subsequent performance of portfolios formed from the selected funds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scalable Counterfactual Explanations for Time Series via Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11704v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating counterfactual (CF) explanations for time series is challenging due to complex temporal dependencies and the computational inefficiency of traditional instance-specific optimization. We propose a policy learning framework for counterfactual explanation in time series, trained as a one-step contextual-bandit policy-learning problem. By training an actor-critic agent in the latent space of a pre-trained autoencoder, our method learns a transferable policy that captures global structural regularities. Unlike existing methods that rely on iterative optimization for each new input, the learned policy enables direct generation of valid and plausible counterfactuals through a single forward pass, avoiding per-instance optimization at inference time. Experiments on 13 UCR benchmark datasets show that our framework achieves high validity and strong plausibility across multiple anomaly detection metrics. Overall, by decoupling counterfactual generation from per-instance optimization, the proposed method provides an efficient and scalable alternative to existing approaches for time series counterfactual explanations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at IEEE Big Data 2026</p></details> |
| **[Tiny but Trusted: Efficient Vision-Language Reasoning for Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30344v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Vision--Language Models (VLMs) have achieved impressive performance across many tasks, yet prior studies report unsatisfactory performance when applying large language or multimodal models to finding abnormal patterns in sequential data. Public anomaly detection benchmarks typically provide interval annotations but not natural-language rationales, making it difficult to fine-tune VLMs to produce grounded, interpretable decisions. To address this gap, we construct VisAnomBench, a curated benchmark built from public time-series datasets and augmented with high-quality anomaly explanations selected from multiple large VLMs using fine-grained, task-specific rewards. Through fine-tuning on this benchmark, we develop VisAnomReasoner, a parameter-efficient VLM for time-series anomaly detection. Results on VisAnomBench show that VisAnomReasoner achieves more accurate anomaly localization and consistently outperforms all baselines, with improvements of at least 21.23 and 23.87 percentage points in precision and F1, respectively. Additional experiments on the TSB-AD-U benchmark demonstrate strong cross-benchmark generalization, with VisAnomReasoner improving precision and F1 by 9.57 and 13.39 percentage points, respectively.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Updated experiments and appendix</p></details> |
| **[Nearest-Neighbor Radii under Dependent Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14343v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Nearest-neighbor methods are fundamental to classical and modern machine learning, yet their geometric properties are typically analyzed under independent sampling. In this paper, we study the nearest-neighbor radii under dependent sampling. We consider strong mixing dependent observations and ask whether dependence changes the scale of nearest-neighbor neighborhoods. We establish distribution-free almost sure convergence under polynomial mixing and sharp non-asymptotic moment bounds under geometric mixing. The moment bounds depend on the local intrinsic dimension rather than the ambient dimension, making the results applicable to high-dimensional data supported on lower-dimensional manifolds. Synthetic experiments and real-world time-series benchmarks illustrate the theory, showing that nearest-neighbor geometry remains informative under dependent sampling.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Language in the Flow of Time: Time-Series-Paired Texts Weaved into a Unified Temporal Narrative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08942v4)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While many advances in time series models focus exclusively on numerical data, research on multimodal time series, particularly those involving contextual textual information, remains in its infancy. With recent progress in large language models and time series learning, we revisit the integration of paired texts with time series through the Platonic Representation Hypothesis, which posits that representations of different modalities converge to shared spaces. In this context, we identify that time-series-paired texts may naturally exhibit periodic properties that closely mirror those of the original time series. Building on this insight, we propose a novel framework, Texts as Time Series (TaTS), which considers the time-series-paired texts to be auxiliary variables of the time series. TaTS can be plugged into any existing numerical-only time series models and effectively enable them to handle time series data with paired texts. Through extensive experiments on both multimodal time series forecasting and imputation tasks across benchmark datasets with various existing time series models, we demonstrate that TaTS can enhance multimodal predictive performance without modifying model architectures. Our Code is available at https://github.com/iDEA-iSAIL-Lab-UIUC/TaTS.</p></details> | ICLR 2026, 47 pages |
| **[GPU-accelerated Regularized Vector Multiplicative Error Models for Positive-valued Financial Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08443v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The logarithmic vector multiplicative error model (log-vMEM) provides a methodology for modeling and forecasting multivariate positive-valued time series. However, the number of autoregressive parameters increases rapidly with the system dimension and lag order, posing challenges for estimation and model selection. This paper develops a regularized estimation framework for the log-vMEM with multivariate Gamma errors by incorporating hierarchical lag structures to induce sparsity and accommodate heterogeneous lag dynamics. We consider three hierarchical lag structures: componentwise, own-other, and elementwise, in combination with group lasso, adaptive group lasso, group MCP, and group SCAD penalties. Parameter estimation is carried out through an alternating block-coordinate optimization algorithm with sequential Gauss-Seidel updates, while GPU-accelerated numerical quadrature is employed to address the computational burden of evaluating the multivariate Gamma likelihood. Simulation experiments investigate parameter recovery and lag selection under different sparsity structures. The real data analysis is carried out on the realized volatility measures for Microsoft (NASDAQ:MSFT) which allows a comparison of the competing regularization approaches in an empirical financial setting. Together, these developments provide a computational framework for sparse multivariate volatility modeling with flexible lag structures and dependent Gamma errors.</p></details> | <details><summary>ISBIS...</summary><p>ISBIS-STATFIN Conference 2026 contributed talk (Conference link: https://statfin.cmi.ac.in/ISBIS2026/, Talk link: https://statfin.cmi.ac.in/ISBIS2026/#:~:text=72-,Rohan%20Chattre,-UConn)</p></details> |
| **[A Survey of Post-Training in Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20002v4)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have emerged as general-purpose models for time series analysis, but pretraining alone is often insufficient for reliable downstream deployment. Bridging this gap requires further intervention to handle domain shift, task heterogeneity, limited supervision, and computational constraints, which motivates post-training as a broad class of methods to adapt, augment, compose, calibrate, or specialize pretrained TSFMs for downstream tasks. In this work, we analyze TSFM post-training methods based on their locus of intervention in the prediction pipeline, yielding five categories: parameter adaptation, context augmentation, model composition, output processing and uncertainty control, and compression and specialization. Within each category, we study main representative methods and discuss their current limitations. We further identify future directions toward controlled adaptation, reliable context construction, uncertainty-aware model composition, calibrated output processing, and deployment-aware specialization. Overall, by providing a unifying framework for the emerging TSFM post-training landscape, this work aims to support future research to navigate the design space between a pretrained TSFM and its reliable downstream deployment.</p></details> | Accepted at TMLR |
| **[ClusLoad: Cluster-Guided Model Sharing for Scalable Distribution Transformer Load Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11170v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale device-level load forecasting for distribution transformers is challenged by substantial cross-device heterogeneity and the deployment burden of maintaining device-specific forecasting models. Existing approaches typically rely either on device-specific models, which are difficult to scale to large transformer populations, or on global models, which reduce deployment complexity but may overlook heterogeneous load patterns across devices. To address this trade-off, this study develops a trend-clustered forecasting framework that enables model sharing among transformers with similar temporal behaviors. Representation Learning for Time Series using Time-Embeddings (T-Rep) is employed to learn compact temporal representations of transformer load series, which are subsequently grouped using a Gaussian mixture model (GMM). Representative transformers are sampled within each cluster to train cluster-specific Patch Time Series Transformer (PatchTST) models while retaining device-level predictions. The framework is evaluated using real-world 15-min operational data from nearly one thousand distribution transformers. The proposed framework reduces mean absolute error (MAE) by approximately 20% relative to the direct PatchTST baseline, achieves a high-load warning accuracy of 95.66%, while requiring only seven cluster-specific forecasting models. These results demonstrate that behavior-based device organization provides an effective balance between forecasting accuracy, deployment scalability, and risk-oriented operational support for distribution transformer management.</p></details> |  |
| **[AnomaMind: Coarse-to-Fine Agentic Diagnosis via Hybrid Inference for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13807v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) supports the analysis of web service key performance indicators (KPIs) by identifying anomalous intervals. Recent large language model (LLM)-based agentic approaches have achieved promising results through reasoning and tool use. Despite their effectiveness, precise anomaly localization remains challenging, as diverse anomaly patterns demand targeted numerical evidence and iterative refinement to accurately determine anomaly boundaries. In this work, we propose AnomaMind, a coarse-to-fine agentic diagnosis framework for TSAD. AnomaMind first identifies suspicious regions from time-series visualizations, then adaptively acquires targeted numerical evidence through multi-turn tool interactions, and finally refines anomaly decisions through self-reflection. This process is supported by a tool engine that integrates knowledge memory and numerical analysis. To coordinate this diagnostic process, we design a hybrid inference strategy that employs frozen general-purpose LLMs for initial localization, tool orchestration, and evaluation, alongside a specialized LLM detector for evidence-grounded interval prediction. We optimize only the specialized detector through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on diagnostic trajectories and reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable detection rewards. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning web traffic, service KPIs, and system-monitoring signals, under both in-domain and multi-domain training settings, demonstrate competitive performance and support the effectiveness of coarse-to-fine adaptive diagnosis through targeted evidence acquisition and self-reflective refinement.The code is available at https://github.com/Xiaoyu-Tao/AnomaMind-TS.</p></details> |  |
| **[CastFSR: Fast--Slow--Reflect Agentic Reasoning for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03031v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series forecasting is fundamental to temporal data mining, yet numerical models often struggle to account for changing contextual factors. A key challenge is determining when contextual evidence should guide forecast revisions while maintaining consistency with temporal patterns and domain knowledge. To address this, we propose CastFSR, a fast--slow--reflect agentic framework for context-aware time series forecasting. Fast-thinking forecasting analyzes numerical patterns, selects a suitable forecaster, and constructs a data-driven forecast prior. Slow deliberative reasoning incorporates relevant contextual evidence to selectively refine this prior, while reflective evaluation checks the revised forecast against temporal patterns, contextual evidence, and domain constraints. A shared state maintains forecasting priors, contextual evidence, intermediate forecasts, and feedback across stages. CastFSR supports both training-free LLM coordination and two-stage post-training for compact LLM coordinators. | **[Beyond Sunny Skies: Using Large Language Models to Predict Urban Traffic in Adverse Weather](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12635v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban traffic flow prediction is a cornerstone of intelligent transportation systems, yet accurate forecasting under adverse weather conditions remains a significant challenge. Existing data-driven models, while effective for routine patterns, often struggle with the sparse data and complex non-linear shifts associated with rare meteorological events. These methods typically treat environmental contexts as shallow numerical features, failing to capture the underlying semantic reasoning-why and how specific adverse conditions alter mobility behaviors across regions with different functional compositions. To address this limitation, we propose Multi-STG, a unified framework that integrates Large Language Model (LLM)-derived semantic priors with graph-based spatiotemporal learning. Multi-STG introduces an LLM-empowered semantic enhancement module that transforms comprehensive context descriptions such as weather, POIs, and holidays into high-level semantic embeddings, effectively providing a knowledge-driven complement to data scarcity. Furthermore, we design a multimodal attentional fusion mechanism to dynamically weigh heterogeneous inputs and a semantic-enhanced prediction head with gated residual calibration to robustly adjust forecasts based on semantic guidance. Extensive experiments on real-world multi-city private car datasets demonstrate that Multi-STG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, reducing MAE by 11.82\% on average relative to the strongest baseline across three cities, while maintaining stable performance under sparse and non-stationary conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Handshakes Go Dark: An Adaptive Semantic-Sequential Fusion Framework for Encrypted Traffic Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12442v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Encrypted traffic classification is essential for network security monitoring and traffic management. Observable upper-layer bytes are therefore dominated by ciphertext, weakening the link between raw-byte patterns and application semantics and limiting model generalization. Moreover, the availability and discriminative value of observable information vary across protocols, protection profiles, and tasks, complicating classification across diverse scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose FLARE, a Field-aware Learning and Adaptive Representation Fusion framework for encrypted traffic classification. FLARE pre-trains a BERT-based packet encoder through Masked Field Prediction, which reconstructs masked tokens from field-structured context. During downstream fine-tuning, the parameters of the pre-trained packet encoder remain frozen, and a packet-level gated module adaptively combines protocol-semantic representations with directional packet-length representations. A flow-level encoder then models inter-packet dependencies for classification. We evaluate FLARE on eight datasets spanning diverse encrypted-traffic tasks, including CipherSpectrum and VisQUIC for strongly encrypted scenarios. FLARE achieves superior performance over baselines across the eight datasets, exceeding the strongest pre-trained baseline by 10.92 percentage points on QUIC website fingerprinting. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of field-aware pre-training and adaptive semantic-sequential fusion under constrained observability.</p></details> |  |

Which axis matters depends on how the corpus was collected, in a way invisible from its label space. We give a diagnostic that identifies the right axis before any model is trained: single-identifier probes. A decision tree on the capture identifier alone reaches 0.983 under a random split and exactly 0.000 under session-disjoint splitting. | **[ICS-Sniper: A Targeted Blackhole Attack on Encrypted ICS Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.06140v5)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern industrial control systems (ICS) increasingly host their Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) services in the cloud to reduce the costs of large-scale automation. We present ICS-Sniper, a targeted blackhole attack that analyzes the VPN traffic metadata (sizes, direction, timing of packets) to identify narrow time windows, called critical superperiods, during which the site-SCADA traffic would likely contain highly critical commands or data. Post-analysis, in a subsequent operational cycle, ICS-Sniper drops a small set of payload-carrying packets in the critical superperiods to disrupt the ICS's operations. We demonstrate three attacks on two realistic modern Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) plant testbeds that can potentially violate the operational safety of the ICS while evading state-of-the-art ICS attack detectors.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 12 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[TrafficInstruct: A Multi-Task Network Traffic Dataset and Benchmark for Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03319v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Instruction tuning for multi-task network traffic analysis requires data with clearly defined sources, supervision targets, and evaluation conditions. We verify sample sources and partition the dataset by complete collection sessions, keeping all samples from the same session within one partition. The benchmark defines allowed inputs, deterministic label mappings, fixed training/validation/test partitions, and task-wise scoring rules. We evaluate majority-class, random-forest, and ET-BERT baselines, an unadapted 27B language model, and independently trained task-specific LoRA adapters on a shared frozen base. | **[MATE: Multi-Agent Traffic Engineering for Topology-Aware Distributed SRv6 Scheduling under Bursty Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01831v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As network traffic becomes increasingly dynamic and bursty, conventional centralized traffic engineering (TE) suffers from long control-loop delays caused by network-state aggregation, centralized computation, and policy distribution. In this paper, we propose MATE, a multi-agent TE method for proactive distributed scheduling in hybrid networks with partial deployment of Segment Routing over IPv6 (SRv6). MATE uses historical traffic stability to characterize the burst risk of each traffic demand and path sensitivity to quantify the ability of candidate paths to accommodate additional traffic, thereby enabling differentiated proactive scheduling tailored to heterogeneous traffic characteristics. MATE adopts an offline-centralized training and online-distributed execution architecture. During training, a shared global critic evaluates joint actions under a reward combining maximum link utilization (MLU) with variability-weighted path sensitivity, while counterfactual difference rewards provide agent-specific credit for actor learning. During online execution, edge agents generate traffic-splitting policies from local observations and apply them through SRv6, avoiding centralized optimization in each scheduling interval. Experiments on the China Environment for Network Innovations (CENI) testbed and simulations on the Sanet topology show that MATE outperforms a multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) ablation baseline without agent-specific credit assignment, COPE, and TEAL in most evaluated scenarios and approaches centralized FIGRET on measured traffic traces. On CENI-Abilene, MATE achieves MLU within 10% of the linear-programming optimum in up to 90.1% of test instances. | **[FLAT: Smoothing the Rugged Landscape for Learnable, Sample-Efficient Traffic Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04337v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Calibrating microscopic traffic models for digital twins is an expensive black-box optimization problem: tuning car-following and lane-changing parameters requires a full simulation run, affording only a tight budget per recalibration window. We present FLAT, which couples what to optimize with where to sample next. An eight-dimensional behavioral fingerprint smooths the parameter-error landscape, making the objective learnable from a few dozen samples; annealed lower-confidence-bound (LCB) acquisition then spends each remaining run where it most reduces error. The surrogate, interchangeable among a Gaussian process (GP), random forest (RF), or multi-layer-perceptron (MLP) ensemble, plugs into the same LCB loop. Across six heterogeneous real-world scenes, FLAT-GP achieves the lowest scene-averaged behavioral error, winning 6/6 scenes against SPSA, GA, and CMA-ES and 5/6 against TPE under the matched budget. Some baselines need up to 4.4 times more simulations to match. Ablations show objective choice shifts final behavioral error by 81% on average, removing sequential LCB raises the six-scene mean by 20%, and surrogate choice shifts it by at most 4.2%, confirming gains trace to objective geometry and sequential allocation rather than surrogate capacity.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 28 figures. To understand how the bargaining process shapes the outcomes, we introduce a behavioral taxonomy that jointly characterizes numerical bidding actions and communicative attitudes. We examine two complementary aspects of social interaction: inter-agent bargaining dynamics and private-to-public behavioral transformation. Results reveal distinct interaction patterns associated with agreement and breakdown, including greater constructive reciprocity in agreement trials and more frequent conflict escalation in breakdown trials. Furthermore, private and public communicative attitudes often differ even when numerical bids remain unchanged. | **[BR-MoE: Budgeted Semantic Release for Privacy-Preserving Traffic Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27026v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic cameras support useful analysis, but their outputs can reveal private information. Replacing images with text reduces direct visual exposure. It does not place a formal bound on disclosure. The published reinforcement learning and mixture-of-experts framework refines scene descriptions through relevance, coverage, and brevity rewards. It does not account for repeated releases under a fixed privacy budget. This paper proposes budgeted semantic release as an extension of that framework. Four scene experts first process isolated video chunks. Their outputs are mapped to a fixed, bounded semantic schema. A reinforcement learning controller selects which expert summaries to release and how much privacy budget to spend. The controller uses public task requests and previous private releases. A separate privacy filter enforces the total budget. Calibrated noise is added before the summaries are converted to text. Under explicit chunk isolation and bounded-event assumptions, the complete release transcript satisfies an event-level differential privacy bound. The design also supports reuse of previous summaries without new disclosure. An evaluation protocol covers traffic utility, privacy attacks, release freshness, and computational cost. Illustrative tables show the intended reporting format. They do not establish empirical gains. The proposed contribution is a task-aware release interface with auditable privacy accounting for repeated traffic descriptions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learned Terminal Costs for Short-Horizon Model Predictive Control of Autonomous Trucks in Interactive Highway Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40257v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous highway driving for heavy-duty trucks is a challenging control problem. In this paper, we propose a hybrid MPC and RL framework for highway driving in autonomous trucks, where a finite-horizon MPC, solved via dynamic programming, uses a pre-trained RL model for terminal cost estimation. We evaluate the framework in a simulation environment with reactive surrounding traffic and compare it against pure MPC with zero terminal cost, rollout with terminal cost estimation using a base policy, and pure RL. Results show that the hybrid approach achieves zero collisions with the lowest cost of any configuration.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 2 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |

| **[Optimization-based lane-changing trajectory planning in mixed traffic with embedded input convex neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38939v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lane-changing (LC) maneuvers are highly challenging vehicle interactions, with substantial implications for traffic safety and efficiency. Both surrogates are embedded via linear programming (LP) formulations, avoiding the binary variables required by conventional mixed-integer programming (MIP) approaches for NN-embedded optimization. We further theoretically establish their exactness at optimality and extend the framework to problems with additional analytical objective terms and multiple surrogates. Experiments on a hybrid dataset combining real-world and simulated trajectories demonstrate that the proposed method achieves surrogate accuracy competitive with unconstrained baselines while substantially improving optimization efficiency over conventional MIPs. | **[Proba: A High-Performance, Low-Traffic Probabilistic Spatial Memory Streaming Prefetcher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30448v3)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial Memory Streaming (SMS) prefetchers exploit recurring spatial access patterns across memory regions to predict future accesses. | **[A Minimally Informed Black-Box Fake-Report Attack on DRL Traffic-Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38557v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) traffic-signal controllers may use vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I) reports, exposing their perception layer to false-data injection. This black-box, minimally informed attacker trains an imitation surrogate of the controller. We demonstrate that despite the loss of accuracy relative to an attacker who can observe the entire state and action space, this limited attacker can significantly increase the average waiting time for other approaches while barely changing the waiting time on her own approach, i.e. externalizing the added delay onto the other approaches. Because the only realizable move is to inflate one's own reported queue, we find that a zero-knowledge attacker that simply reports the maximum feasible queue is as damaging as the surrogate-based one, while an unstructured random inflation is not: the surrogate is not required to reproduce the degradation, and the exposed vulnerability is the V2I sensing channel itself. The surrogate's role is directional control, selecting a disruptive or a selfish objective at the same budget. The attack persists under +/-20% uniform demand variation. Finally, on a common per-lane budget we place the attacker on a knowledge ladder against white-box adversaries, and find the black-box surrogate matches a realizable white-box attacker that uses the victim's own gradients; only abandoning realizability raises the damage further.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. | **[GAS-PFN: Graph-Aware Spatiotemporal Prior-Data Fitted Networks for Zero-Shot Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37598v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic forecasting requires modeling complex temporal dynamics and the spatial dependencies imposed by road topology. Empirical reliability and ablation analyses further assess robustness to component failures and geometric orientation. SIGMA integrates language-guided objective adaptation, multi-faced policy learning, and statistical reliability assessment into an adaptive framework for intelligent traffic control.</p></details> |  |
| **[She Spoofed Sea Ships by the Sea Shore: Measuring Large-Scale GPS Spoofing in Global Maritime Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37676v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GPS spoofing has emerged as a serious threat to maritime security, yet its global prevalence, persistence, and structure remain largely unmeasured. In this paper, we present the first large-scale measurement study of maritime GPS spoofing, using global Automatic Identification System (AIS) data, which contain the GPS coordinates broadcasted over time by ships across the world. We focus on large-scale regional spoofing, where external interference displaces many vessels across an area at once, leaving a recognizable signature of physically implausible motion correlated across ships; our motion-aware, marine-specific framework identifies this signature and grades the evidence for GPS spoofing in each region it finds. Applying our approach to AIS data from over 367,000 vessels collected between late November 2024 and early February 2025, we identify 31 persistent anomalous hotspots across high-traffic maritime regions, at least 22 of which show strong evidence of GPS spoofing, with spatial and temporal structure aligning with regional conflict and economic sanctions. Notably, our method found that the spoofing activity in the Red Sea responsible for the highly-publicized grounding of the 75,000-ton container ship, MSC Antonia, was ongoing months before the incident, which has not been previously documented. Similarly, we detected persistent spoofing in the Strait of Hormuz over a year before the 2026 Iran war brought commercial shipping through the Strait to near-standstill. | **[Risk Control of Traffic Flow Through Chance Constraints and Large Deviation Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01321v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We develop a rare chance-constrained optimal control framework for autonomous traffic management, with traffic dynamics governed by stochastic partial differential equations. To efficiently enforce these probabilistic safety specifications, we exploit a large deviation theory (LDT) based approximation method, which converts the original highly non-convex, sampling-heavy stochastic optimization problem into a tractable deterministic nonlinear programming problem. The proposed LDT-based reformulation exhibits superior computational scalability, as its computational cost remains approximately constant regardless of the target violation probability, as opposed to the scaling challenge of traditional sampling-based methods. The effectiveness of the proposed framework is illustrated through numerical experiments on a ring road with stochastic on-ramp inflow, and evaluated with two representative risk measures, namely, maximum traffic density and speed variation. The results show that the proposed method keeps the violation probability close to the target without parameter tuning and is more than ten times faster than a risk-averse baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Squeeze-Aware-YOLO: Early Channel Recalibration and Multi-Scale Fusion for Real-Time Traffic Sign Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15604v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic sign detection plays a vital role in intelligent transportation systems, supporting the safe operation of advanced driver assistance systems and autonomous vehicles. To address these challenges, we propose Squeeze-Aware-YOLO, a lightweight traffic sign detection framework that introduces a multi-branch bottleneck module combining convolutional kernels of different sizes with a Squeeze-and-Excitation mechanism to improve feature extraction and adaptive channel weighting. In addition, we integrate the Multi-Branch Auxiliary Feature Pyramid Network and Re-parameterized Heterogeneous Multi-Scale modules to strengthen multi-scale feature representation and feature fusion while preserving real-time inference efficiency for practical deployment. The proposed approach is evaluated on the Bangladeshi Traffic Sign Detection Dataset, the CSUST Chinese Traffic Sign Detection Benchmark 2021, and the German Traffic Sign Detection Benchmark, achieving mAP@0.5 scores of 74.7%, 79.4%, and 84.0%, respectively, while consistently outperforming recent state-of-the-art YOLO-based traffic sign detectors. These findings demonstrate that combining multi-scale receptive fields, channel attention, and enhanced feature fusion enables more accurate and robust traffic sign detection while maintaining the computational efficiency required for real-world deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at DICTA 2026 (International Conference on Digital Image Computing: Techniques and Applications, Sydney, Australia). 8 pages, 7 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[PACC: Protocol-Aware Cross-Layer Compression for Compact Network Traffic Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08331v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Network traffic classification underpins network security and management but faces growing challenges from pervasive encryption and evolving protocols. Existing representations involve clear trade-offs: hand-crafted flow statistics are efficient but lossy, raw-bit encodings can be accurate but costly, and pre-trained embeddings support transfer but often flatten the protocol stack and entangle cross-layer signals. We observe substantial redundancy both across and within network layers, which existing paradigms do not explicitly address, wasting capacity and encouraging shortcut learning that degrades generalization. To address this, we propose PACC, a redundancy-aware cross-layer representation framework. PACC models protocol layers as multiview inputs and learns faithful, compact projections factorized into shared (cross-layer) and private (layer-specific) components. It incorporates an information-preserving sparse segment representation to reduce structural repetition and supervised intra-layer filtering to suppress task-irrelevant semantic redundancy. A behavior-guided shared/private decomposition aligns cross-layer consensus while retaining layer-specific information, supported by information-theoretic analysis. Across encrypted application classification, IoT device identification, benign/malicious traffic classification, and Tor traffic detection, PACC achieves the best overall performance. On encrypted traffic, it achieves an F1 score of 0.9685, substantially outperforming existing baselines while using approximately 15x fewer computes and less GPU memory than the latest pre-trained traffic model.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoSignal: LLM-Guided Evolutionary Design of Modular Traffic Signal Control Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09563v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective traffic signal control (TSC) requires policies that respond to changing traffic demand and network conditions while meeting different control objectives. However, adapting existing strategies often involves repeated manual design and adjustment, making it difficult to systematically explore better control rules for a target network. Large language models (LLMs) can automate this process, but directly using them to select signal phases leaves decision rules embedded in black-box models and incurs recurring inference costs and latency. This paper formulates TSC as a modular program design problem and proposes EvoSignal, an LLM-guided evolutionary framework using traffic knowledge and performance feedback. The modular representation separates traffic feature extraction, local phase prioritization, and optional network-based priority adjustment. Starting from several established strategies, EvoSignal improves programs through feedback on congestion and signal operation, retaining strategies with different performance trade-offs. The resulting programs operate without online LLM inference. A program prioritizing travel time and queue length outperforms all 20 baselines on all three metrics in the search scenario and remains among the top three on each metric when transferred unchanged to the other four scenarios. These findings support automated design of inspectable control programs that transfer across the evaluated road networks and traffic demands. | **[FlowWise: Stateful Fast-Slow Model Serving for Streaming Traffic Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03694v3)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Network traffic analysis in cloud and internet service provider (ISP) networks increasingly relies on machine learning (ML) models, as encryption and service consolidation make rule-based classification unreliable. Unlike typical cloud inference workloads, packets from the same flow arrive over time and require per-flow state. This streaming, stateful setting limits naive scale-out with stateless workers. This paper presents FlowWise, a serving system for streaming, stateful flow intelligence on commodity CPU/GPU servers that jointly selects (i) how many packets to collect and (ii) which model(s) to apply for each flow to balance latency, service rate, and accuracy. Across three representative tasks, inference time across candidate models differs by 1.8x-141.3x, while packet waiting time can exceed feature computation and inference by 6-8 orders of magnitude at the tail. FlowWise exploits these gaps with an uncertainty-driven fast-slow cascade: it issues early predictions from a one-packet model and escalates only uncertain flows to slower models that wait for additional packets and use richer features. A queue-centric runtime maintains per-flow state, performs asynchronous feature joining, and uses brokered, flow-ID-keyed streams to distribute workload across heterogeneous workers. FlowWise serves 76.3% of flows within 16 ms, improving median end-to-end serving latency by 40.5x while increasing service rate and maintaining similar accuracy to always running the slow model. With thousands of packet-header features per flow, our scale-up benchmark sustains over 48.5k new flows per second on a single 16-core commodity CPU server, comparable to reported flow-arrival rates in city-backbone traces. FlowWise is open sourced at https://github.com/noise-lab/FlowWise.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by SoCC 2026</p></details> |
| **[RIECNN: Real-Time Image Enhanced CNN for Traffic Sign Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15127v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic sign recognition plays a crucial role in the development of autonomous cars to reduce the accident rate and promote road safety. In this paper, we introduce Real-Time Image Enhanced CNN ({\our}) for Traffic Sign Recognition. {\our} is a real-time, novel approach that tackles multiple, diverse traffic sign datasets, and out-performs the state-of-the-art architectures in terms of recognition rate and execution time. Experimental results show that our approach has achieved the highest recognition rate for all Benchmarks, achieving a recognition accuracy of 99.75\% for GTSRB, 99.25\% for BTSC and 99.55\% for rMASTIF. In terms of latency and meeting the real-time constraint, the pre-processing time and inference time together do not exceed 1.3 ms per image. Not only have our proposed approach achieved remarkably high accuracy with real-time performance, but it also demonstrated robustness against traffic sign recognition challenges such as brightness and contrast variations in the environment.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in Neural Computing and Applications, Volume 34, pages 6085-6096 (2022)</p></details> |

## Graph Neural Networks
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Transpilation-Aware Runtime Prediction for Noisy Quantum Circuit Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12980v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting the runtime of noisy quantum circuit simulations is important for scheduling, resource allocation, and performance optimization. However, accurate prediction is challenging because backend-aware transpilation can substantially alter the original circuit structure, while the backend-derived noise model and simulator execution behavior can introduce additional runtime variation. We study the effectiveness of graph neural networks (GNNs) and conventional regression methods in predicting Qiskit Aer simulation runtime measured after transpilation. We construct a dataset from a benchmark pool of 1,402 unique circuits spanning 22 circuit families, two Qiskit fake-backend configurations, and four transpiler optimization levels. Specifically, we compare a source GNN using original circuit information, hybrid GNN combining source-level graph with post-transpilation features, and transpiled GNN using only transpiled circuit information, along with five regression models. In the overall-model setting, the transpiled GNN achieves the strongest performance among the graph-based representations at all four optimization levels, obtaining $R^2$ values of 0.974, 0.713, 0.745, and 0.605 for optimization levels 0 through 3, respectively. However, under backend-specific evaluation, the advantage of GNN decreases, with conventional regression models matching or outperforming the GNNs in several settings. These results indicate that post-transpilation information is useful, while the value of explicit graph modeling depends on the backend and optimization level.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in the Proceedings of the 2026 AAAI Fall Symposium Series (Second AAAI Symposium on Quantum Information & Machine Learning)</p></details> |
| **[QT-Net: Rethinking Evaluation of AI Models in Atomic Chemical Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10458v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Atomic properties such as partial charges or multipoles encode chemically meaningful information that can inform downstream molecular property prediction, but their evaluation as machine learning targets has been complicated by the absence of a principled out-of-distribution evaluation protocol at the atomic level. In this work, we propose a held-out evaluation protocol that clusters atomic environments by SOAP descriptors and computes metrics accounting only for cluster labels unseen during training. Following this procedure, we use 5$\times$5 cross-validation and Tukey's HSD to run a statistically rigorous comparison of E(3)-equivariant against non-equivariant, rotationally augmented models for predicting electron populations and multipoles of H, C, N, and O atoms. Building on our results, we introduce the Quantum Topological Neural Network (QT-Net), a rotationally augmented, non-equivariant graph neural network. We show that QT-Net can be used to infer properties of atoms in molecules from QM9 outside our training set, and that the atomic environments seen during training strongly affect the performance of molecular property prediction tasks. To further validate the framework, molecular dipole moments computed from QT-Net's per-atom outputs recover the ground-truth values reported in QM9. We release all code and data, including a JAX implementation of QT-Net.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pricing Symbolic Rules by What GNNs Leave Unlearned](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12446v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Symbolic knowledge (motif counts, ring counts, label constraints) is routinely added to graph neural networks (GNNs) and justified by their limited expressivity. We replace this yardstick with an exact one. The largest log-loss reduction a rule $S$ can give a model family is $I(Y;S\midσ)$ given what the family computes; every realised gain splits exactly into this term plus repaid neural debt; and for a trained network with representation $T$ the value is $I(Y;S\mid T)$, the label information training failed to extract. Rule design thus becomes rule discovery: we score candidates on the network's out-of-fold residual against calibrated permutation nulls with family-wise error control. On ZINC, discovery recovers the hidden ring term $\max(0,\ell_{\max}-6)$ of the target in three independent runs ($p_{\mathrm{adj}}=0.001$) and beats expert cycle counts; frozen and applied to five unseen datasets it is never significantly worse than the plain network or the expert rule, and on BBBP it significantly beats both.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, 10 tables. Accepted at the GraphML Workshop (Graph Machine Learning: Foundations, Frontiers, and Applications) at the 18th Asian Conference on Machine Learning (ACML 2026), Melbourne, Australia</p></details> |
| **[Equivariant Spherical Transformer for Efficient Molecular Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23086v5)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Equivariant Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have significantly advanced the modeling of 3D molecular structure by leveraging group representations. EST explicitly mixes information across sampled orientations, unlike prior orientation-wise spherical operations, while retaining a strong approximate equivariant inductive bias through near-uniform spherical sampling. As demonstrated by our experiments on challenging benchmarks like OC20, MPtrj and QM9, EST-based models achieve state-of-the-art performance. We characterize the ideal closed-domain condition under which the continuous operation is exactly equivariant and empirically quantify the finite-sampling accuracy--equivariance--memory trade-off used in practice.</p></details> | 31 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Reconstructing hourly traffic on every street of a capital from a sparse counting network: a graph neural network applied to Paris](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12379v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Micro-scale air quality models require hourly traffic data on every road segment, whereas permanent counters cover only a small part of the network. The model adapts the Graph Neural Network for Urban Interpolation (GNNUI) to a real, sparse counting network. Flows are decoded on the edges of the junction graph (one edge per segment, both directions summed), a traffic climatology serves as an offset in a zero-inflated negative-binomial likelihood, and training proceeds by masking counters. Eight-fold spatial cross-validation, with counters sharing a published series merged before splitting, distinguishes interpolation at a withheld counter in an instrumented district (R${}^2$ = 0.89, error of a quarter of the mean flow) from extrapolation to a district without counters (R${}^2$ = 0.74, a third of the mean flow). These scores hold on main axes where counters are located, while relative errors are about 50% on the few counted local streets. Ablations show that the climatology offset tends to help extrapolation. Road attributes, time cycles and the live state of the counting network matter most, while weather inputs have no significant effect. | **[Interpretable Context-Aware Multi-Label Retinal Classification with Dual-Hub Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09309v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-label retinal screening requires capturing co-occurring pathologies and clinical context, yet standard multimodal fusion remains opaque. | **[How Hard Is It for Message-Passing GNNs to Simulate One Step of the Weisfeiler-Lehman Test?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.01308v7)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Message-passing graph neural networks (MPGNNs) are commonly compared with the Weisfeiler-Lehman (WL) test, but this comparison does not quantify the resource parameters a network needs to implement a step of the WL test with bounded-size messages and finite numerical precision. We study the cost of simulating a single step of the WL test on unattributed graphs. We distinguish input-independent, or oblivious, simulation from instance-dependent simulation. In the former, the parameters, or their distributions in randomized models, are fixed before the input instance is known. Our results show that the local update rule of the WL test hides a global relabeling problem. In the oblivious setting, deterministic and zero-error randomized MPGNNs cannot solve this problem in the worst case using only shallow networks with small messages. We complement this lower bound with a nearly matching construction in a stronger rooted, port-aware model. By contrast, when the color set is large, bounded-error randomness can greatly reduce the cost, and a one-layer MPGNN with messages of logarithmic size and a logarithmic number of random bits suffices. We show that this logarithmic number of random bits is essentially necessary for shallow, small-message simulations. When the color set is small, we still obtain a rooted, port-aware simulation, but this construction requires more layers or larger messages. We also prove that this extra cost is partly unavoidable, as small color sets force a nontrivial trade-off between the number of layers and the message size. Finally, instance-dependent simulation can be much shallower, but the required instance-specific parameters are not necessarily easy to find. Together, these results reveal quantitative structure hidden behind the statement that MPGNNs match the WL test.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Enhancing Multi-Step Chemical Mechanism Prediction with Graph-Guided Preference Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12013v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting multi-step chemical reaction mechanisms requires identifying plausible intermediates and correctly ordering the elementary reaction steps. Third, when OOD prediction error exceeds ID error, the AUROC of distance-based and density-based detectors correlates with the size of that gap. This explains the non-monotonic relationship between GNN depth and generalization, providing new insights into oversmoothing research. | **[What Carries Architectural Signal? ## Contrastive Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[COMET: Codebook-based Online-adaptive Multi-scale Embedding for Time-series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01635v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time-series anomaly detection aims to identify abnormal temporal patterns in complex real-world systems. However, robust unsupervised time-series anomaly detection remains challenging because anomalies can manifest over different temporal ranges and emerge from interactions among variables, while distribution shifts can cause normal patterns to drift over time. To address these challenges, we propose Codebook-based Online-adaptive Multi-scale Embedding for Time-series anomaly detection (COMET), a unified framework with three tightly coupled components. First, Multi-scale Patch Encoder learns correlation-aware patch embeddings by combining variable-specific temporal dynamics with shared inter-variable structure across multiple patch scales. Second, Vector-Quantized Coreset quantizes these embeddings into representative normal prototypes, which are used to detect anomalies through a dual score combining quantization error and density-aware memory distance. Third, Online Codebook Adaptation leverages the same codebook structure to identify reliable normal samples from training-activated codebook entries, and then updates the prototypes through contrastive learning at inference time. Experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that COMET achieves the best performance on 39 out of 45 evaluation metrics compared to seven baselines, showing robust performance across diverse evaluation protocols.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster). 31 pages, 5 figures, 23 tables. Code: https://github.com/JINU6497/COMET</p></details> |
| **[VETime: Vision Enhanced Zero-Shot Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16681v4)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series anomaly detection (TSAD) requires identifying both immediate Point Anomalies and long-range Context Anomalies. To resolve this dilemma, we propose VETime, the first TSAD framework that unifies temporal and visual modalities through fine-grained visual-temporal alignment and dynamic fusion. VETime introduces a Reversible Image Conversion and a Patch-Level Temporal Alignment module to establish a shared visual-temporal timeline, preserving discriminative details while maintaining temporal sensitivity. Furthermore, we design an Anomaly Window Contrastive Learning mechanism and a Task-Adaptive Multi-Modal Fusion to adaptively integrate the complementary perceptual strengths of both modalities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that VETime significantly outperforms state-of-the-art models in zero-shot scenarios, achieving superior localization precision with lower computational overhead than current vision-based approaches.
